B11: Key proceedings:
B12: 1.Registration before Monday, Aug 17th, 09:00 UTC is mandatory for participation in any part of the meeting.
B13: 2.RAN1#126 is an ordinary face-to-face meeting which counts for the determination of voting rights for TSG RAN. Please remember that check-in for RAN1 #126 is required between the start and the end of the meeting.
B14: 3.Participants of this meeting are encouraged to avoid attachments via the RAN1 email reflector in order to not degrade the email reflector performance:-before the meeting: upload your Tdocs via 3GU-during the meeting: upload your Tdocs to the Inbox folder on the meeting server.
B15: 4.Recordings of any parts of the present meeting is strictly prohibited. No individual or entity - including the speakers and/or the authors -may electronically record any portion of the meeting without prior written consent of the RAN Chair and all the RAN meeting participants.
B16: Opening of the meeting (Day 1: 9:00 am)
B17: Call for IPR
B18: I draw your attention to your obligations under the 3GPP Partner Organizations' IPR policies.  Every Individual Member organization is obliged to declare to the Partner Organization or Organizations of which it is a member any IPR owned by the Individual Member or any other organization which is or is likely to become essential to the work of 3GPP.
B20 R0: The attention of the delegates to this meeting was drawn to the fact that 3GPP Individual Members have the obligation under the IPR Policies of their respective Organizational Partners to inform their respective Organizational Partners of Essential IPRs they become aware of. The delegates were asked to take note that they were thereby invited: to investigate whether their organization or any other organization owns IPRs which were, or were likely to become Essential in respect of the work of 3GPP.  to notify their respective Organizational Partners of all potential IPRs, e.g., for ETSI, by means of the IPR Statement and the Licensing declaration forms ( http://www.etsi.org/WebSite/document/Legal/IPRForms.doc ).
B22: Competition Law Statement
B23 R0: The attention of the delegates to this meeting was drawn to the fact that 3GPP activities are subject to antitrust and competition laws and that compliance with said laws is therefore required of any participant of this WG meeting including the Chair and Vice Chairs. In case of question, please contact your legal counsel. The present meeting will be conducted with strict impartiality and in the interests of 3GPP. Furthermore, delegates were reminded that timely submission of work items/contributions in advance of WG meetings is important to allow for full and fair consideration of such matters.
B25: Network Usage Conditions
B26 R0: Users shall not use the network to engage in illegal activities. This includes activities such as copyright violation, hacking, espionage or any other activity that may be prohibited by local laws. Users shall not engage in non-work related activities that consume excessive bandwidth  or cause significant degradation of the performance of the network. Since the  network is a shared resource , users should exercise some basic etiquette when using the 3GPP network at a meeting. It is understood that high bandwidth applications such as downloading large files or video streaming might be required for business purposes, but delegates should be strongly discouraged in performing these activities for personal use. Downloading a movie or doing something in an interactive environment for personal use essentially wastes bandwidth that others need to make the meeting effective. The meeting chairman should remind end users that the network is a shared resource; the more one user grabs, the less there is for another. Email and its attachments already take up significant bandwidth (certain email programs are not very bandwidth efficient). In case of need the chair can ask the delegates to restrict IT usage to things that are essential for the meeting itself. Don’t place your WiFi device in ad-hoc mode  Don’t set up a personal hotspot in the meeting room  Do try 802.11a if your WiFi device supports it  Don’t manually allocate an IP address  Don’t be a bandwidth hog by streaming video, playing online games, or downloading huge files  Don’t use packet probing software which clogs the local network (e.g., packet sniffers, port scanners)
B28: Consensus Principles Reminder
B29 R0: Decision PCG54/10 :  PCG approved to incorporate the following text to the agendas of each and every TSG and Working Group on “Consensus principles reminder”: The attention of the delegates to the meeting is drawn to the fact that 3GPP endeavours to reach consensus on all decisions and therefore depends on a cooperative spirit of the Individual Members. In particular, Individual Members are encouraged to seek a consensus-based solution and only to sustain objections as a very last resort, and where absolutely necessary and well justified. The leadership will conduct the present meeting in a manner whereby informal methods of reaching consensus are encouraged, whilst ensuring that well justified concerns are taken into account.
B30: Streamlined Standards
B31 R0: Endorsement RAN#107 :  RAN endorsed the following working principle for 6G (in RP-250766): 3GPP to create lean and streamlined standards for 6G, e.g., by dimensioning an appropriate set of functionalities, minimizing the adoption of multiple options for the same functionality, avoiding excessive configurations, etc. Any exception to the above shall be well justified.
B33: Check-in for Registered Delegates
B34 R0: The attention of the delegates to this meeting was drawn to the fact that it is not permitted to check in other delegates on their behalf. In the event of technical difficulties preventing check-in, delegates should present themselves in person to the Secretary.

B222 R0: Reason for change: | Current 3GPP specifications do not clarify the application time of the  differential k-offset  MAC-CE in NB-IoT NTN. Issues have been encountered during interoperability testing resulting from this lack of clarity.
B222 R1:  | 
B222 R2: Summary of change: | Clarify that the differential k-offset applies after subframe  n+13,  where  n  is the last subframe of the NPDSCH carrying the MAC-CE. Clarify that  the applicable k-offset is the one active at the first subframe of the DCI for an NPUSCH transmission, and the first subframe of the NPDSCH for a TA command.
B222 R3:  | 
B222 R4: Consequences if not approved: | Potential misalignment between UE and eNB on the application time of the differential k-offset.
B224: 16UE Procedures related to narrowband IoT
B225: Throughout this clause,
B226: -for a NB-IoT UE in a IoT NTN TDD serving cell,
B227: -the UE shall not assume any downlink physical signal or physical channel is present in any subframe other than within the D consecutive downlink subframes according to the frame structure type 1 for IoT NTN TDD and the value of D defined in [3],
B228: -the UE shall not transmit any uplink physical signal or physical channel in any subframe other than within the U consecutive uplink subframes according to the frame structure type 1 for IoT NTN TDD and the value of U defined in [3].
B229: -the term “DL subframe” includes the 𝐷 consecutive downlink subframes, the 𝑈 consecutive uplink subframes and guard period subframes as defined in [3].

B334 R0: Reason for change | Incorrectly capture of previous agreement. 
B334 R1: Summary of change | To align with the  agreement  made in RAN1#122, remove  “ consecutive ”  from CPU occupancy duration for SP CSI-PAI report.

B382: Reason for change: In Rel-19, UEIRI is introduced as a new type of UCI for notifying (ModeA) or requesting a PUSCH resource (ModeB) for UE-initiated/event-driven beam reporting. Whether UE transmits UEIRI on a PUCCH format 0 or format 1 in the PUCCH resource depends on whether the triggering condition of the event(s) for UE-initiated/event-driven beam reporting is met or not. In this case, the information bit of UEIRI is not always included in the PUCCH resource. However, in the current spec, UEIRI is described as a compulsory UCI in the PUCCH resource, which is not correct.
B383: Summary of change: Add the condition “if any” to the information bit for UEIRI where the contents of UCI bit(s) are described in the first paragraph of Section 9.2, TS38.213.
B384: Consequences if not approved: The availability of information bit for UEIRI in PUCCH is not correctly stated.

The HARQ-ACK information bits correspond to a HARQ-ACK codebook as described in clause 9.1. B452 R0: Reason for change:   OD-SIB1 configuration configures search space 0 and CORESET 0 for NES Cell. The set of symbols of CORESET 0 in a slot is used for downlink reception and should not be configured as uplink by  sib1-tdd-UL-DL-ConfigurationCommon  or  tdd-UL-DL-ConfigurationDedicated . The current specification is not clear for this case while it is clear for other cases (e.g., CORESET 0 configured by  pdcch-ConfigSIB1  in MIB). Summary of change:   Clarify the symbols of CORESET 0 of NES Cell configured by OD-SIB1 configurations cannot be configured as uplink. B459 R0: Reason for change:  The periodicity of on-demand SSB can be configured differently than the always-on SSB. However, the current spec says the UE can assume a periodicity of a half frame when the always-on SSB periodicity parameter is absent. When the on-demand SSB periodicity is different than the always-on SSB periodicity this then conflicts with the following statement in the spec which says the UE can assume the periodicity of all SS/PBCH blocks in the serving cell is the same.  Summary of Change:  Clarify that the assumption of the same periodicity from all SS/PBCH blocks in the serving cell does not hold for on-demand SSB. Consequences if not approved:   Incorrect UE assumption on the periodicity of the SSB.  4.1 Cell search < Unchanged Text Omitted > A UE can be  provided  per serving cell by  ssb - periodicityServingC ell  a periodicity of the half frames for reception of  the  SS/PBCH blocks  for the  serving cell. B478 R0: Spec | TS 36.213
B478 R1: Reason for change: | In NB-IoT NTN TDD operation, it was concluded to do not “specify further enhancements for NPUSCH format 1 scheduling delays”. This was adopted based on the understanding that legacy scheduling values (8, 16, 32, 64) can be used under the assumption that if the scheduled uplink transmission falls into a guard period subframe (muted subframe) then the most immediate available subframe is used. However, this assumption/behavior is currently not described into the 3GPP technical specifications.
B478 R2:  | 
B478 R3: Summary of change: | Incorporating in clause 16.5.1 of TS 36.213 for NB-IoT NTN TDD operation, a statement  that  aligns terminology of legacy procedures (i.e., “a slot is considered an NB-IoT UL slot if is it fully contained within an NB-IoT UL subframe”) to reflect that if because of the scheduling delay value the NPUSCH format 1 transmission happens to coincide with a guard period subframe, then the NPUSCH format 1 transmission shall be performed at the first available subframe of the  U  consecutive uplink subframes of NB-IoT NTN TDD operation.
B478 R4:  | 
B478 R5: Consequences if not approved: | If the behavior is not described, there is no guarantee that all implementations will have to assume that a scheduled uplink transmission that falls into a guard period subframes should be understood to be postponed to the most immediate available uplink subframe.
B478 R6: Clauses affected | 16.5

B487: R1-2606834
B488: Agreement:
B489: Capture the following in the Draft LS response to R1-2605188.
B490: The misalignment between transmission of PRACH at UE side and monitoring of PRACH at NW side may occur in beam hopping operation when RO periodicity is <160ms. Some companies have the view that the misalignment is due to configuration or NW implementation. 
B491: The misalignment may also occur in beam hopping operation when RO periodicity is 160ms and cells served by the same hopping beam have overlapping valid PRACH occasions, e.g. due to the aligned SFN and same PRACH time-domain configuration and SSB-to-RO mapping, while the corresponding satellite Rx beam is not active for a footprint at such PRACH occasions.

Otherwise, a slot is counted in the number of    slots  if the symbols allocated for the transmission occasion in the slot are all non-SBFD symbols and  not include a DL symbol indicated by  tdd-UL-DL-ConfigurationCommon  or  tdd-UL-DL-ConfigurationDedicated , if provided, or a symbol of an SS/PBCH block with index provided by  ssb-PositionsInBurst . - For PUSCH repetition type B, UE drops an actual repetition if the actual repetition is not in the valid symbol type. - otherwise, the UE transmits the PUSCH occasions in SBFD symbols and non-SBFD symbols after applying  collision handling in clause 11.1 of [6, TS 38.213], if any . B578: R1-2606746FL summary of LS discussion on R20 LBCA via switchingModerator (Apple)
B580: Agreement:
B581: For Rel-20 LBCA via switching, in terms of interpretation of configured new Rel-20 switching pattern 
B582: For a bit value of ‘0’ for a first slot in the bitmap, keep the same interpretation as Rel-19, i.e., UE can transmit/receive on the PCell in the first slot.
B583: For a bit value of ‘1’ for a second slot in the bitmap, change the interpretation, i.e., UE can receive on both the SCell and the PCell simultaneously in the second slot and cannot transmit on the PCell.
B585: Agreement:
B586: Introduce new RRC parameter for Rel-20 LBCA via switching including a switching pattern, a gap duration for switching from PCell UL to SCell DL, and gap duration for switching from SCell DL to PCell UL.
B587: The value range of each RRC parameter in Rel-20 LBCA via switching configuration is the same as the value range of the corresponding parameter in Rel-19 LBCA via switching configuration.
B588: ‘0’ in the switching pattern indicates operation on FDD DL/UL carrier (PCell) only
B589: ‘1’ in the switching pattern indicates dual-DL operation on FDD DL carrier + SDL carrier (SCell)
B590: A UE cannot be configured simultaneously with both the Rel-19 LBCA via switching and the Rel-20 LBCA via switching.
B591: The same as Rel-19, no UE feature related to Rel-20 LBCA via switching is expected in RAN1
B593: Agreement:
B594: For Rel-20 LBCA via switching, in terms of collision handling, when Rel-20 LBCA via switching is configured, only the following modification to the existing Rel-19 LBCA via switching collision is needed 
B595: UE can receive SS/PBCH block, PDSCH, or PDCCH, or CSI-RS, or PRS configured by higher layers on the PCell in a set of symbols of a slot with bit value ‘1’, unless the set of symbols includes at least one symbol that is in the second duration.
B596: UE can expect to detect a DCI format scheduling or triggering a PDSCH, or PDCCH, or CSI-RS, or PRS reception on the PCell in a set of symbols of a slot with bit value of ‘1’, unless the set of symbols includes at least one symbol that is in the second duration.

For each candidate cell,  the UE determines the  time domain behavior of a SS/PBCH block from  ssb-Periodicity  and  ssb-PositionsInBurst  and the frequency domain  behavior  of a SS/PBCH block is determined by the higher layer parameters  subcarrierSpacing ,  ssb-Frequency . - A  LTM-NZP-CSI-RS-ResourceSet  comprises of a list of Z  ≥ 1 NZP CSI-RS resource   indices (given by  ltm -CSI-RS-ResourceList ) and a list of Z  LTM-CandidateIds  (given by  ltm-CandidateIdList ) referring to  candidate  cells associated with the  NZP CSI-RS resource   indices .  The UE shall expect that  LTM-NZP-CSI-RS-ResourceSet  are configured with the higher layer parameter  repetition  set to  ‘ off ’  when  LTM-ReportContent  configured within the  LTM-CSI-ReportConfig  associated with the LTM CSI Resource Setting is set to ‘ cri-RSRP ’ . **unchanged omitted** 5.2.1.4.2 Report quantity configurations **unchanged omitted** If a UE is configured with a  ltm-CSI-ReportConfig  with  ltm-ReportConfigType  which is set to ‘periodic’ or ‘semiPersistentOnPUCCH’ or ‘semiPersistentOnPUSCH’ or ‘aperiodic’ , - if the UE is configured with  spCellInclusion  with  reportQuantity  set to 'ssb-Index-RSRP', the UE shall report in a single reporting instance  nr O fReportedRS -PerCell   different SSBRI   for the current SpCell and each of the  nrO fReportedCells -1   candidate  cells. B952: Improvement of SRS capacity and coverage
B954: R1-2605711Moderator summary on improvement of SRS capacity and coverage: Round1Moderator (CATT)
B957: Conclusion: 
B958: Regarding whether an SRS resource set spans two slots or is confined within one slot,
B959: If the offset values of all the SRS resources within the SRS resource set are k' = 0, 
B960: when all the SRS resources are entirely contained within the S slot, the SRS resource set is confined within one slot
B961: when at least one SRS resource crosses a slot boundary, the SRS resource set spans two slots.
B962: If the SRS resources within the SRS resource set have different k' values (both 0 and 1), the SRS resource set spans two slots.
B963: If the offset values of all the SRS resources within the SRS resource set are k' = 1, the SRS resource set is confined within one slot.

B1107: The remaining part of broadcast information is transmitted with
B1108: Transmission periodicity: integer multiple (>1) of Psync

B1129: R1-2605364FL summary #4 for 9.3.1 Ambient IoT R2D design aspects in Rel-20Moderator (Huawei)
B1131: Agreement:
B1132: For R2D periodic sync signal and sequence-based SIP, 
B1133: For R2D periodic sync signal
B1134: The number of binary sequence(s) is 3
B1135: Manchester coding is applied to binary sequence
B1136: For sequence-based SIP
B1137: The number of binary sequence(s) is 3 for a give length
B1138: (Working assumption) Manchester coding is not applied to binary sequence
B1140: Agreement:
B1141: For R2D periodic sync signal, a pre-defined sequence length is 255
B1143: Agreement:
B1144: For R2D sequence-based SIP, the maximum sequence length is 511 (based on working assumption)
B1146: Agreement: 
B1147: For R2D periodic transmission of broadcast information, 
B1148: The transmission periodicity of the remaining part of broadcast information is indicated by the first part of broadcast information
B1149: Multiple integer values are supported, with a maximum value of 16 
B1150: FFS: detail values
B1152: Agreement: 
B1153: For R2D, for the transmission of periodic unmodulated sinusoid single tone CFO calibration signal
B1154: The periodicity is to be selected from the followings
B1155: Option P1: same as the pre-defined transmission periodicity (Psync) of R2D periodic sync signal
B1156: Option P2: integer multiple (>1) of Psync and same as transmission periodicity of the remaining part of broadcast information
B1157: The time location(s) is to be selected from the followings
B1158: Option T1: immediately after a periodic R2D sync signal transmission including padding if any
B1159: Option T2: immediately after a first part of periodic broadcast information transmission including padding if any
B1160: Option T3: immediately after a remaining part of periodic broadcast information transmission including padding if any
B1162: Agreement: 
B1163: For the time duration of unmodulated sinusoid single tone CFO calibration signal transmission, at least 14 OFDM symbols duration including CP is selected
B1164: FFS other duration if necessary
B1165: Note: the time duration does not include time gap placed at the start and at the end if any

B1229: Agreement:
B1230: For PDRCH scrambling sequence initialization, Device ID-related information is used for cinit calculation except for Msg1 in CBRA and first D2R transmission for DO-A. 
B1231: For Msg1 in CBRA and first D2R transmission for DO-A, the device uses 0 as the device ID-related information input.
B1233: Agreement:
B1234: For PDRCH scrambling sequence initialization, Device ID-related information refers to the AS ID defined in TS 38.391, i.e., the AS ID used to address a specific device for R2D reception and D2R scheduling.
B1236: Agreement:
B1237: Regarding when to (re)initialize the scrambling sequence generator for PDRCH, the scrambling sequence generator is initialized at the beginning of the PDRCH, and a scrambling sequence is generated over the entire PDRCH transmission.
B1239: Agreement:
B1240: For Rel-20 D2R preamble, determine the supported sequence lengths from {7, 31, 63, 127, 255} based on the following alternatives:
B1241: Alt 1: All 5 sequence lengths are supported.
B1242: Alt 2: 4 sequence lengths are supported.
B1243: Alt 2-1: Removal of 7
B1244: Alt 2-2: Removal of 127
B1245: Alt 2-3: Modulation-dependent removal of one sequence length, if applicable.
B1247: Agreement:
B1248: For Rel-20 D2R midamble structure for BPSK, Option 2 structure is used as baseline, while Option 1 structure can be additionally enabled or disabled by the reader via D2R scheduling information.
B1249: Option 1: The entire generated midamble sequence is inserted at each midamble position.
B1250: Option 2: The generated midamble sequence is divided, and one bit of the sequence is inserted at each midamble position.
B1251: FFS: midamble at the end of the PDRCH
B1253: Agreement:
B1254: For D2R FDM(A), Option 1, where the D2R frequency resource is determined based on a reference frequency (fref), an offset (foffset) and Tb, is supported.
B1256: Agreement:
B1257: For determination of the reference frequency (fref), companies are encouraged to provide a comparison between Alt 1 and Alt 2 considering the following aspects:
B1258: For Alt 1, 
B1259: Alt 1-1: the reference frequency indicated by the reader is based on NREF (i.e., AIoT-ARFCN) defined in TS 38.191 Clause 5.4.1.1, requiring X bits for indication.
B1260: Alt 1-2: the reference frequency indicated by the reader is based on D2R channel raster according to RAN4 discussion, requiring Y bits for indication.
B1261: For Alt 2, 
B1262: fR2D_sync_raster ​is determined by the device from the received R2D synchronization signal.
B1263: foffset,2 ​has a range of [A, B] MHz and requires C bits for indication to the device. 
B1264: FFS: A, B, C, including whether A, B are band-dependent.
B1266: Agreement:
B1267: The maximum number of D2R block-level repetitions is 16.
B1269: Agreement:
B1270: For Rel-20 D2R preamble, three sequences are supported for each preamble length.

B1811: R1-2606772FL summary#2 for 6G channel codingModerator (ZTE)
B1814: Observation:
B1815: For the study of 5G RM code for small UCI with 3~11 bits, the following are observed based on companies’ contributions:
B1816: 11 sources (R1-2605455, R1-2605560, R1-2605655, R1-2605714, R1-2605783, R1-2606019, R1-2606283, R1-2606307, R1-2606308, R1-2606371, R1-2606638) observed that 5G RM code suffers from error floor and performance degradation at high code rates (e.g., R>=1/2) due to rank deficiency or limited minimum Hamming distance
B1817: On the other hand, the following are also observed:
B1818: 2 sources (R1-2605288, R1-2606793) observed small UCI with 3~11 bits is generally scheduled with low code rates.
B1819: 1 source (R1-2605821) observed that the rank-deficiency cases occur in approximately 6.7% of all the valid (E, K) configurations and have negligible impact on system performance. 
B1820: 5 sources (R1-2605288, R1-2605455, R1-2605560, R1-2605714, R1-2605783) observed that the mother code length of 5G RM code is limited to 32. Furthermore, 4 sources (R1-2605288, R1-2605455, R1-2605560, R1-2605783) observed repetition is needed at low code rates, thus the performance and coverage is degraded.  1 source (R1-2605288) observed small UCI with 3~11 bits is generally scheduled with low code rates.
B1821: 4 sources (R1-2605455, R1-2605560, R1-2606308, R1-2606371) observed the BLER performance loss of 5G RM code due to DMRS overhead at least for PUCCH.
B1822: 2 sources (R1-2605455, R1-2605560) observed the performance loss of 5G RM code due to high PAPR at least for PUCCH.
B1823: 3 sources (R1-2605455, R1-2605560, R1-2605909) observed 5G RM code cannot support DMRS-less transmission at least for PUCCH due to the all-one vector in the first column.
B1824: In addition, the following are also observed:
B1825: 4 sources (R1-2605821, R1-2606793, R1-2606769, R1-2605379) observed that RM code was used in 5G NR, and 6G does not have new requirements for this region to justify re-design.
B1826: 1 source (R1-2605379) observed that 5G RM code is optimized for good minimum distance and can be decoded via FHT-based ML decoding with low complexity.
B1827: The potential solutions (coding based only) to enhance performance for small UCI with 3~11 bits are summarized as below
B1828: 12 sources (R1-2605560, R1-2605655, R1-2605714, R1-2605783, R1-2605909, R1-2606793, R1-2606283, R1-2606307, R1-2606308, R1-2606371, R1-2606638, R1-2606019) discussed the potential solutions to improve the performance for short block code.
B1829: Enhanced RM code
B1830: 8 sources (R1-2605560, R1-2605655, R1-2605714, R1-2605783, R1-2606793, R1-2606371, R1-2606638, R1-2606019) observed enhanced RM code with optimized basis sequence selection and/or rate matching can resolve the rank deficiency and error floor issue of RM code at high code rates.  
B1831: 1 source (R1-2605783) observed enhanced RM code can provide 0.7dB and 1.4dB performance gain under the case of K=3 and 4 at medium code rate respectively, resolve error floor or provide 0.7~1.2dB performance gain under the case of K=6~8 and 11 at higher code rate.
B1832: 1 source (R1-2605714) observed that enhanced RM code by introducing row permutation in the basis RM sequence selection can resolve error floor under the cases of K=6~9 and 11 at higher code rate, and provide 0.52~0.57 dB performance gain under the cases of {E=12, K=6, 7} and {E=14, K=7}.
B1833: 1 source (R1-2606371) observed applying an interleaver before rate matching for 5G RM code can fix the rank deficiency problem, and provide 0.1~0.9dB, 0.1~0.4dB and 0.1~0.2dB performance gain for K = 3~11, E = 12, 16 and 24, respectively.
B1834: 1 source (R1-2606019) observed Kerdock code, which contains the first-order RM code, can provide 0.11dB mean performance gain, 2.62dB maximum performance gain across all combinations of (K, E).
B1835: 1 source (R1-2606793) observed extension of RM code (n*32, K), n=2 or 4 can provide up to 0.6 dB performance gain in AWGN for n=4 over 5G RM code (32, K) with repetition.
B1836: 1 source (R1-2605909) observed that RM (32,13) code agreed in RAN1 #52bis can provide 11 base sequences, excluding the base sequence of all-1’s.
B1837: Enhanced PC-Polar code
B1838: 5 sources (R1-2605288, R1-2605560, R1-2605714, R1-2605783, R1-2605909) observed that enhanced PC-Polar code can resolve the rank deficiency and error floor issue of 5G RM code at high code rates, and improve the performance at low code rates
B1839: 1 source (R1-2605909) observed about 0.7dB gain from enhanced PC-Polar code for K=8 bits and E=256 bits.
B1840: 1 source (R1-2605288) observed enhanced PC-Polar code can improve the BLER performance by [0.6~0.9] dB and increase coverage by >10% over 5G RM code at low code rates, and also observed enhanced PC-Polar code can provide the benefits of error detection via parity check bits and unequal error protection for important bits.
B1841: vertical and horizontal code
B1842: 1 source (R1-2606308) observed vertical and horizontal code can provide about 0.4 dB performance gain for K=8, E=12, and about 2 dB performance gain for K=8, E=26.
B1843: New code derived from linear block codes or orthogonal subspaces
B1844: 1 source (R1-2606283) observed by constructing the short block length codes using sequences or using generator polynomial, the minimum hamming distance can be increased.
B1845: CRC polynomial code
B1846: 1 source (R1-2606307) observed CRC polynomial code can provide better BLER performance than 5G RM code for K=6, E=8, and K=7, E=10.
B1847: Golay-based code
B1848: 1 source (R1-2606371) observed Golay-based code can provide 0.1~1.0dB, 0.2~0.6dB, 0.1~0.4dB, 0.1~0.4dB and 0.2~0.3dB performance gain for K = 3~11, E= 12, 16, 20, 24 and 32, respectively.
B1849: On the other hand, 5 sources (R1-2605379, R1-2605821, R1-2606793, R1-2606769, R1-2606494) suggested reusing 5G RM code for small UCI with 3~11 bits, e.g., to avoid optimization for high coding rates that are atypical for normal usage.
B1850: 2 sources (R1-2605821, R1-2605379) observed that the rank-deficiency (E, K) issue can be resolved by the scheduler through allocating a marginally larger number of coded bits without specification change.
B1853: R1-2606773FL summary#3 for 6G channel codingModerator (ZTE)

B2043: Conclusion:
B2044: With respect to RANP task to RAN1 (copied below) to conclude the support for constellation shaping in RAN1:
B2045 R0: Conclusion (RAN#112) RAN1 to continue the study on GS (i.e. 1D-NUC) and PS (ESS based PS and ECC based PS in Q3/26 decide by Q3 whether or not to support constellation shaping and if supported, between PS (including whether it is 1-bit or 2-bits PS scheme) and GS support of different constellation shaping schemes for DL and UL is precluded (e.g. PS for DL, GS for UL) If there is no consensus on the support of constellation shaping in Q3/26 the study on constellation shaping will be stopped
B2046: RAN1 concludes the following:
B2047: There is no consensus in RAN1 to support any constellation shaping schemes for either DL or UL.
B2048: Summary of companies’ preferences is in R1-2606762.
B2049: Some proponents of some constellation shaping schemes stated that they would agree to withdraw that scheme.

B2075: Agreement
B2076: For further study of initial access for idle mode multi-carrier operation, consider at least the following potential use cases:
B2077: UC-1: UL coverage enhancement / DL-UL coverage imbalance
B2078: UC-2: RACH capacity / offloading / load balancing
B2079: UC-3: Network energy saving / common-signaling reduction
B2080: Other use cases are not precluded.
B2081: For each identified use case, study whether and under which conditions multiple-carrier operation in idle mode provides additional benefit over existing mechanisms.
B2083: Agreement
B2084: For study of multiple carriers where for initial access a UE can utilize resources for UL (Msg1, etc) or for both UL (Msg1, etc.) and DL (Msg2, etc.) in one carrier other than the carrier where the UE obtains the system information providing configuration for those resources, consider the following potential scenarios for the set of candidate physical carriers for which resources are provided for initial access:
B2085: S1: 1 DL physical carrier + N UL physical carriers, where N > 1
B2086: S2: M DL physical carriers + 1 UL physical carrier, where M > 1 
B2087: S3: M DL physical carriers + N UL physical carriers, where M > 1 and N > 1
B2088: Note 1: The carrier where the UE obtains the system information and other candidate physical carrier(s) are counted above.
B2089: Note 2: For a given UE and a given initial access procedure, UE may use only a subset of the candidate physical carrier.
B2090: Note 3: The inclusion of a scenario for study does not imply agreement to support it. The need and applicable use cases/deployment scenarios for each scenario are subject to further study.

B2094: As a baseline, a single selected physical carrier is used per link direction throughout an attempt of an RACH procedure.

B2306: 1. Overall Description:
B2307: In the study of “Re-use of existing 5G mid-band (~3.5GHz) site grid for 6G deployments in at least around 7 GHz and targeting comparable coverage to 5G mid-band”, RAN1 made an agreement that for the link budget evaluations in around 7 GHz for coverage gap identification, the assumptions for the receiver noise figure are 9 dB and 5 dB for DL and UL respectively. There is no implication that RAN4 should use the same assumption.

B2332: For the MaxCL requirement in TR 38.914, companies are encouraged to provide MaxCL evaluations based on the following assumptions:
B2333: Assumptions used for maxCL calculations in TR38.914: 
B2334: Table 5.1.20.1-2: MaxCL calculation assumptions
B2335 R0: UE Tx power | 23dBm
B2335 R1: DL Tx power | 46dBm
B2335 R2: BS   receiver noise figure | 5dB
B2335 R3: UE receiver noise figure | 9dB
B2335 R4: Interference margin | 0dB
B2336: Note: DL Tx power is 46dBm per 20 MHz and scales with wider bandwidth. 
B2337: Carrier frequency, duplex and SCS
B2338: FDD, carrier frequency: 0.7 GHz, 15kHz SCS
B2339: TDD, carrier frequency: 2.6 GHz, 30kHz SCS
B2340: UE speed: 3kmh
B2341: Channel model
B2342: TDL-C with 300ns delay spread
B2343: DL: 2T2R and 2T1R
B2344: UL: 1T2R 
B2345: Signals/channels to be evaluated
B2346: DL: NR PSS/SSS, PBCH, Common PDCCH, SIB1 PDSCH, Msg2/4 PDSCH
B2347: UL: NR PRACH format C2/B4/0, MSG3 PUSCH, MSG5 PUSCH (companies to report assumption of the payload of actual transmission),  MSG4 PUCCH Format 1
B2348: The simulation assumptions in section A.1 in TR38.830 are used as a starting point. 
B2349: The payload for NR SIB1 PDSCH is 1200 bits.

B2356: Agreement 
B2357: For SSB transmission, study
B2358: Option 1:
B2359: For a given frequency band, a single set of values for {X, Y, M} is predefined in default 6GR SSB periodicity
B2360: Non-adjustable number of repetitions of transmitted SSBs
B2361: Note: for discussion purpose, 
B2362: X: the max number of candidate 6GR SSBs in a 6GR SSB burst
B2363: Y: the max number of candidate 6GR SSB burst(s) in a default 6GR SSB periodicity
B2364: M: the max number of candidate 6GR SSB repetitions within a default 6GR SSB periodicity
B2366: Option 2:
B2367: For a given frequency band, a single set of values for {X, Y, M} is predefined in default 6GR SSB periodicity and the actual number of repetitions of transmitted SSBs can be adjusted by the aNB
B2368: FFS: how to adjust the actual number of repetitions of transmitted SSBs including potential restrictions
B2369: Note: for discussion purpose,
B2370: X: the max number of candidate 6GR SSBs in a 6GR SSB burst
B2371: Y: the max number of candidate 6GR SSB burst(s) in a default 6GR SSB periodicity
B2372: M: the max number of candidate 6GR SSB repetitions within a default 6GR SSB periodicity
B2374: Agreement
B2375: RAN1 to study and evaluate the following solutions 
B2376: Option 1: 
B2377: SSB repetition within one SSB period and UE is assumed to at least support SSB combining within one SSB period
B2378: 160 ms default SSB periodicity for initial cell search 
B2379: Option 2: 
B2380: SSB repetition within one SSB period and UE is assumed to at least support SSB combining within one SSB period
B2381: 80 ms default SSB periodicity for initial cell search 
B2382: Option 3: 
B2383: UE is assumed to at least support SSB combining across different SSB periods
B2384: 40 ms default SSB periodicity for initial cell search 
B2386: Option 4: 
B2387: SSB repetition within one SSB period and UE is assumed to at least support SSB combining within one SSB period and support SSB combining across different SSB periods
B2388: 40 ms default SSB periodicity for initial cell search
B2389: Option 5: 
B2390: UE is assumed to at least support SSB combining across different SSB periods
B2391: 20 ms default SSB periodicity for initial cell search 
B2392: To mitigate the UE impact with longer SSB periodicity in Option 1/2/3/4, RAN1 to study the following solutions
B2393: Solutions to reduce cell search complexity and latency
B2394: Additional synchronization signals
B2395: Clustered allocations of SSB, SIB1 and paging
B2396: Other solutions are not precluded
B2397: FFS: Additional configurable SSB periodicities
B2399: Agreement

B2476: Conclusion
B2477: To report the following details for PRACH evaluations:
B2478: Receiver algorithm details, including:
B2479: Detection threshold strategy:
B2480: threshold configured to address false alarm only (impacting miss detection)
B2481: Example 1) Threshold is determined based on noise variance
B2482: threshold configured to address false alarm (impacting miss detection) and false detection probability jointly
B2483: Example 2) Threshold is determined based on noise variance, inter-sequence interference + noise estimated from measurements outside the window of the detected peak
B2484: Detection metric is normalized by interference+noise estimate, where the estimate is derived (e.g., maximum or median of the detection metric) from the detection metric outside the window around the detected peak within the NCS duration of the preamble. The detection peak window can be +/- estimated/approximated channel delay.
B2485: Example 3) Threshold is determined based on noise variance, sequence interference estimated from measurement of total receive signal power of a RO
B2486: In case of ZC sequence detection with Restricted Set A and B, details of detection algorithm to handle the detection sidelobes.
B2487: For False alarm statistics, which option used for false alarm (and determination of detection threshold)
B2488: Option 1) if BS performs detection on a RO, the false alarm event happens for a preamble sequence detection, and a false alarm event is counted for the preamble sequence (false alarm event may happen multiple times within a RO for across the multiple preamble sequences). In this case, the denominator used to compute false alarm probability is the number of preambles to be detected (per RO).
B2489: Option 2) if BS performs detection on a RO, the false alarm event happens for any preamble sequence detection, and the a single false alarm event counted for a RO. In this case, denominator used to compute false alarm probability is 1 (per RO).
B2490: Relative Tx power assumption of UEs (and any pathloss measurement and RF impairment modeling, if used)
B2491: User distribution per RO, for example
B2492: Same number of users transmitting PRACH preamble in each RO
B2493: Varying number of users transmitting PRACH preamble in each RO (may be based on traffic characteristics)
B2494: Preamble sequence selection
B2495: For example, NR ZC provides information on number of root sequences, number of cyclic shifts, and Ncs value among the 64 preamble set used for evaluation.
B2497: Agreement
B2498: For study of whether the maximum number of preambles per RO is increased beyond 64, take into account at least:
B2499: the justification and use case for the capacity increase (e.g., load model, access rate per cell, target collision probability, availability of ROs in time/frequency);
B2500: the available root-sequence pool and the resulting inter-cell reusing for the assumed sequence length; and
B2501: Note: Preamble sequence, format, as well as availability of ROs in time/frequency are taken into account when calculating available sequence pool and reuse.
B2502: the resulting Miss detection, Type-1 (intra-cell), and Type-2 (inter-cell) false-detection performance under PRACH transmission load in the network.
B2503: Companies are asked to provide information on detection algorithm and detection metric thresholds (and adaptation strategies, if any) used for evaluations.
B2504: associated transmitter/receiver complexity, and random access latency

B2700: Agreement:
B2701: At least aggregation levels of 1, 2, 4, 8, 16 are supported for 6GR PDCCH.
B2702: Note: A CCE aggregation level is not used when the number of CCE in a CORESET is smaller than the aggregation level.
B2705: R1-2606612Feature Lead summary #2 on PDCCH Transmission SchemesModerator (Nokia)
B2707: Agreement:
B2708: A CCE consists of 6 REGs for 6GR PDCCH
B2709: REGs in a REG bundle are contiguous in frequency and time.
B2710: At least REG-bundle size of 6 REGs is supported
B2711: FFS a need for other sizes.
B2712: Agreement:
B2713: For the study of precoding granularity consider the following options from the UE perspective:
B2714: Option 1: Precoding granularity is only a single REG bundle
B2715: Option 2: The same precoder is used for all contiguous REGs in a CORESET
B2716: Option 3: The same precoder is used for multiple contiguous REG bundles in time/frequency within the PDCCH candidate
B2717: Option 4: The same precoder is used for multiple contiguous REG bundles in time/frequency.
B2719: R1-2606613Feature Lead summary #3 on PDCCH Transmission SchemesModerator (Nokia)
B2722: Agreement:
B2723: Both contiguous and non-contiguous frequency-domain resource allocation are supported for 6GR CORESET.
B2724: At least 6-PRB 6GR PDCCH CORESET frequency-domain resource allocation granularity is supported
B2725: FFS a need, feasibility and conditions for other CORESET granularities 
B2728: R1-2606614Feature Lead summary #4 on PDCCH Transmission SchemesModerator (Nokia)
B2730: Agreement:
B2731: PDCCH payload bit-level scrambling before modulation is used for 6GR PDCCH.
B2732: FFS: Details
B2734: Agreement:
B2735: Companies are encouraged to provide the analysis for comparison of MU-MIMO schemes (based on orthogonal and/or non-orthogonal DMRS) and single-layer, single-port, single-user PDCCH considering
B2736: The necessity to introduce orthogonal DMRS for 6GR PDCCH
B2737: At least the following two cases:
B2738: Case1: double resources for two paired MU-MIMO UEs than for a single user and the same total power are assumed from NW point of view
B2739: Case 2: the same resources for single user and two paired MU-MIMO users and the same total power are assumed from NW point of view
B2740: Performance based on link level and/or system level evaluations (e.g., BLER, spectrum efficiency) 
B2741: Used models and criteria on the user pairing for MU-MIMO based on orthogonal multi-port DMRS and/or non-orthogonal single-port DMRS
B2742: Assumptions on the UE receiver
B2743: Assumptions on UE specific precoding
B2744: Impacts on UE complexity
B2745: Assumed channel model (at least CDL channel model for link level evaluations)
B2746: Assumed DCI size
B2747: Note: The discussion of the utilization of orthogonal DMRS ports for other purposes (e.g. intercell-interference mitigation) than MU-MIMO is not precluded.

Agreement : For evaluation on coherent UL MIMO based PUSCH transmission for 6GR, at least the RF coherence requirements for coherent UL MIMO defined in TS38.101-1/2 are used as baseline  Companies to report  Other additional RF coherence requirements can be reported by companies (optional) TX coherency error model
B3203: RAN1 are currently using the coherent UL MIMO requirements in TS38.101-1/2 as baselines for evaluations including coherence imperfections. RAN1 would like to know if the amplitude/phase errors in these requirements will be revisited for 6G, especially for devices that support more than two port UL MIMO transmission. 
B3204: RAN1 have not so far agreed to models of coherence imperfections for codebook evaluations, and so such models are determined by each company. Therefore, while RAN1 can continue investigations, RAN1 would welcome suggestions on amplitude/phase error modeling for 6G UEs so that these may be used to refine RAN1’s evaluations.

B3300: For performance evaluation of maximum number of ports of CSI-RS for CSI acquisition in 6GR, the following is observed for around 7GHz
B3301: Under eType II-like CSI feedback assumption
B3302: For 256 ports
B3303: [3] sources show 256-port provides [+5 ~ +20]% system-level throughput gain compared with 128-port at cell average
B3304: [1] sources show 256-port provides [+3 ~ +34]% system-level throughput gain compared with 128-port at cell edge
B3305: [1] source shows 256-port provides [+5]% system-level throughput gain compared with 128-port at cell average, and [62]% system-level throughput loss compared with 128-port at cell edge
B3306: [1] source shows 256-port provides [+18]% system-level throughput gain compared with 128-port at cell center
B3307: For 512 ports
B3308: [1] source shows 512-port provides [+15 ~ +31]% system-level throughput gain compared with 256-port at cell average, while [1] source shows 512-port provides [7]% system level throughput loss  ~ [+1]% system-level throughput gain compared with 256-port at cell average
B3309: [1] source shows 512-port provides [+31 ~ +55]% system-level throughput gain compared with 256-port at cell edge
B3310: [1] source shows SRS based CSI acquisition suffers [+26 ~ +72]% SE loss compared with 512-port CSI-RS
B3311: Under Type I-like CSI feedback assumption
B3312: For 256 ports
B3313: [3] sources show 256-port provides [+5 ~ +25]% system-level throughput gain compared with 128-port at cell average
B3314: [3] sources show 256-port provides [+5 ~ +61]% system-level throughput gain compared with 128-port at cell edge
B3315: [1] sources shows 256-port provides [+8]% system-level throughput gain compared with 128-port at cell average, and [50]% system-level throughput loss compared with 128-port at cell edge
B3316: [1] source shows 256-port provides [+1 ~ +17]% system-level throughput gain compared with 128-port at cell center
B3317: [1] source shows 256-port causes up to [60 ~ 84]% link-level throughput loss compared with 128-port at -10dB SNR. The loss shrinks and vanishes when SNR increases, and [+2 ~ +3]% gain is observed after -6 or -4 dB SNR, depending on CDL modelling. 
B3318: [1] sources shows the link-level SNR loss of 256-port compared with 128-port is smaller than [0.3] dB at low SNR range
B3319: [1] source shows 256-port provides [3]% link-level SE loss compared with 128-port, whereas the required SNR of 128-port and 256-port converge to reach the same 1% BLER operation point
B3320: For 512 ports
B3321: [2] sources show 512-port provides [10]% system-level throughput loss ~ [+3]% system-level throughput gain compared with 256-port at cell average
B3322: [1] sources show 512-port provides [6]% system-level throughput loss ~ [+3]% system-level throughput gain compared with 256-port at cell edge
B3323: [1] sources show 512-port provides [8]% system-level throughput loss ~ [+2]% system-level throughput gain compared with 256-port at cell center
B3324: [1] source shows 512-port provides [+22 ~ +52]% system-level throughput gain compared with 256-port at cell average
B3325: [1] source shows 512-port provides [+51 ~ +86]% system-level throughput gain compared with 256-port at cell edge
B3326: [1] source shows 512-port provides [2]% link-level SE loss compared with 256-port, whereas the required SNR of 512-port is [2] dB higher than 256-port to reach the same 1% BLER operation point
B3327: Under ideal CSI feedback assumption
B3328: For 256 ports
B3329: [4] sources show 256-port provides [+10 ~ +47]% system-level throughput gain compared with 128-port at cell average
B3330: [4] sources show 256-port provides [+10 ~ +77]% system-level throughput gain compared with 128-port at cell edge
B3331: [1] sources show 256-port provides [0 ~ +35]% system-level throughput gain compared with 128-port at cell center
B3332: For 512 ports
B3333: [2] sources show 512-port provides [+4 ~ +19]% system-level throughput gain compared with 256-port at cell average
B3334: [1] source show 512-port provides [+3 ~ +33]% system-level throughput gain compared with 256-port at cell edge
B3335: [1] sources show 512-port provides [63]% system-level throughput loss compared with 256-port at cell edge
B3336: [1] sources show 512-port provides [0]% system-level throughput gain compared with 256-port at cell center
B3337: Note: for each individual source, the above results are from a same number of antennas at BS side, when comparing different numbers of ports.
B3338: Note: the above results do not model UCI error in the simulations.

B3461: Observation 
B3462: For the potential PAPR impact and potential solutions for PAPR reduction for JSCM-based CSI compression, the following options have been studied in RAN 1, until RAN 1 #126: 
B3463: Option 0: Compression outputs are directly mapped to DFT-s-OFDM or CP-OFDM uplink transmission as modulation constellation without any additional operation
B3464: Option 1: Compression outputs are mapped to certain discrete constellation, and then mapped to DFT-s-OFDM or CP-OFDM uplink transmission, at least considering the following as the discrete constellation
B3465: NR modulation constellations, e.g., 16QAM, 64QAM, 256QAM, 1024QAM
B3466: Other modulation constellations, e.g., 64APSK, constellation point pruning from NR QAM, other customized (fixed) constellations 
B3467: Note: To reduce PAPR (by achieving uniform distribution of JSCM symbols), regularization can be additionally considered as part of loss function as implementation
B3468: Note: the training may or may not be aware of the mapping. 
B3469: Option 2: Compression outputs are mapped/restricted, e.g., with restricted amplitude(s), and then mapped to DFT-s-OFDM or CP-OFDM uplink transmission as modulation constellation
B3470: Note: on top of Option 0/1/2, PAPR restriction during the training with or without additional activation layer, as part of loss function, can be considered. 
B3471: Other solutions or potential issues related to PAPR can be further studied.

B3535: Observation
B3536: The following observations on PAPR/Cubic metric/output power variation (cross OFDM symbols) have been made for the above options at least for projection-matrix-based compression for JSCM, until RAN 1 #126:
B3537: With DFT-s-OFDM as uplink waveform,  
B3538: For Option 0: 13 sources showed without any additional operation, PAPR @CCDF= 0.1% of JSCM increases by 1.5~2dB compared to conventional 16-/64-/256-QAM constellation, and ~3dB compared to conventional QPSK constellation. 
B3539: 4 sources showed that with Kronecker-based matrices, the PAPR @CCDF= 0.1% additionally increases by 1~2.5dB. 
B3540: 1 source showed with PAPR aware training, the PAPR @CCDF= 0.1% is ~0.2dB lower or 0.1dB higher compared to conventional 16-QAM. 
B3541: 1 source showed with PAPR aware training, the PAPR @CCDF= 0.1% is ~0.3dB lower compared to conventional 256-QAM. 
B3542: 1 source showed clipping aware training, the PAPR @CCDF= 0.1% is 9dB. 
B3543: 1 source showed that with PAPR aware training towards 6RB, the PAPR @CCDF= 0.1% is about ~6dB (comparable to QPSK), however, if the number of RBs used in inference is 1RB, the PAPR @CCDF= 0.1% increased to 8dB (i.e., 2dB higher than QPSK).
B3544: 1 source showed that JSCM followed by 2-stage PAPR reduction (first stage trainable constellation mapper followed by second stage adaptive PAPR reduction block), the PAPR @CCDF= 0.1% can be reduced to 4dB (PAPR = 8dB @CCDF= 0.01%).
B3545: 1 source showed the cubic metric @CCDF=0.1% increases ~2.9dB compared to conventional QPSK. 
B3546: 1 source showed JSCM output power variation across OFDM symbols is larger than 1K-QAM PUSCH with assumption of frequency first, time second mapping of JSCM outputs to allocated REs (on to 12 subcarriers in freq domain). 
B3547: For Option 1: 13 companies showed that the PAPR can be reduced but remain higher than conventional QAM
B3548: By mapping to conventional constellations, e.g., 64-QAM or 256-QAM, 
B3549: 13 sources showed PAPR @CCDF= 0.1% can be reduced but remain 0.2~1dB higher than conventional 64-QAM/256-QAM, 
B3550: 1 source showed PAPR @CCDF= 1% can be ~0.5dB lower than conventional constellation, i.e., 64-QAM/256-QAM with scaling of the compression outputs before mapping to the discrete constellations and the training is not aware of the mapping.  
B3551: 1 source showed with regularization as part of loss function and then mapping to 64-QAM, the PAPR is comparable to conventional 64-QAM. 
B3552: 2 sources showed that with Kronecker-based matrices, the PAPR @CCDF= 0.1% is ~0.2 dB higher than conventional 64-QAM (12 subcarriers and 1024 FFT, or 12 subcarriers and 128 FFT). 1 source showed that with Kronecker-based matrices, the PAPR @CCDF= 0.1% is ~1.5dB higher than conventional 64-QAM (64 subcarriers with 2048-FFT). 
B3553: 1 source showed, that varying the number of Resource Elements (REs) impacts the PAPR:
B3554: Dedicated model: with 32 REs, the PAPR at CCDF = 0.1% can be ~0.5 dB lower than that of a conventional 64-QAM constellation. However, with 48 or 64 REs, the PAPR is either comparable to or ~0.3 dB higher than conventional 64-QAM.
B3555: Scalable model: With 32 REs, the PAPR is comparable to conventional 64-QAM. However, with 48 or 64 REs, the PAPR at CCDF = 0.1% can be ~0.7 dB or 1.8 dB higher, respectively, than conventional 64-QAM.
B3556: 1 source showed that different loss functions during training may lead to differences in the statistical distribution/probability of the mapped constellation points from the compression outputs. Specifically, the PAPR @ CCDF = 0.1% might vary by 0.3 dB to 1 dB.
B3557: By mapping to other modulation constellations, 
B3558: 1 source showed with mapping to 64APSK, the PAPR @CCDF= 0.1% is 0.2 dB higher than conventional 64-QAM.  
B3559: 2 sources showed with mapping to constellation point pruning from NR QAM, PAPR @CCDF= 0.1% is comparable or up to 1dB lower comparing to conventional constellation, i.e., 64-QAM or 256-QAM. 
B3560: For Option 2: By mapping/restricting, some companies showed that the PAPR can be reduced to comparable to conventional QPSK or 16-QAM
B3561: 4 sources showed with mapping to constant amplitude, PAPR is comparable to conventional QPSK and 2 sources showed PAPR @CCDF= 0.1% is ~0.5dB lower than conventional QPSK. 
B3562: 2 sources showed with mapping to annular constellation, with scaling factor = 0.5, the PAPR is comparable to conventional 16-QAM. 
B3563: 1 source showed that with mapping to annular constellation, with dense matrix or with Kronecker-based matrices, the PAPR @CCDF= 0.1% is reduced to 8.5dB.
B3564: 1 source showed with both the maximum and minimum amplitudes, the PAPR @CCDF= 1% is comparable to conventional QPSK. And with peak limited only, the PAPR @CCDF= 1% is ~0.4dB higher than conventional 16-QAM.
B3565: Note: no multiplexing with other UL transmission is assumed in the above evaluation. 
B3566: Note: 12 to 72 of subcarriers are assumed with 128, 1024 or 2048 or 4096 FFT size. 
B3567: With CP-OFDM as uplink waveform,  
B3568: For Option 0(without and additional operation) and. for Option 1, by mapping to conventional constellations, e.g., 64-QAM or 256-QAM, 
B3569: 5 sources showed the PAPR of JSCM is comparable to conventional 16-/64-/256-QAM. 
B3570: 1 source showed for 1 RB, the PAPR @CCDF= 0.1% is 1dB higher than conventional 16-/64-/256-QAM. 
B3571: 1 source showed the cubic metric @CCDF=0.1% increases ~1.9dB compared to conventional QPSK
B3572: For Option 1, 
B3573: By mapping to conventional constellations, e.g., 64-QAM or 256-QAM, 
B3574: 4 sources showed the PAPR of JSCM is comparable to conventional 16-/64-/256-QAM. 
B3575: By mapping to other modulation constellations, 
B3576: 1 source showed with mapping to constellation point pruning from NR QAM, PAPR is comparable conventional QPSK, or conventional 16-/64-/256-QAM. 
B3577: For Option 2: By mapping/restricting, 
B3578: 1 source showed with mapping to constant amplitude or annular constellation the PAPR is comparable to conventional QPSK/16-QAM. 
B3579: 1 source showed with both the maximum and minimum amplitudes, the PAPR @CCDF= 1% is about 7.2dB (2.8dB lower than conventional QPSK). And with peak limited only, the PAPR is comparable to conventional QPSK/16QAM.
B3580: Note: no multiplexing with other UL transmission is assumed in the above evaluation. 
B3581: Note: 12 to 72 of subcarriers is assumed with 1024 or 2048 FFT size. 
B3582: Note: in the above observations, for conventional modulations for both DFT-s-OFDM and CP-OFDM waveform, no additional PAPR reduction technique is considered. 
B3583: Note: the observation can be further updated based on new results in future meetings.

B3707: [Post-126-6G- Aspects of UL based CSI acquisition]
B3708: Email discussion for aligning evaluation assumptions and evaluation cases for SRS sequence design from 31st  Aug. to 16th Sept.
B3711: Observation
B3712: For 6GR SRS sequence, the following candidate options are observed:
B3713: Option 1: Enhanced ZC sequence design based on sampling a piecewise LFM chirp at a rate higher than the sweeping bandwidth, e.g., [R1-2606382]
B3714: Option 2: Time-domain/Frequency-domain design e.g., Costas-based \ sequence design, e.g., [R1-2605321]
B3715: Option 3: Sequence construction via concatenation of segments of ZC sequence, with each rotated by a per-segment phase offset, e.g., [R1-2605724]
B3716: Option 4: Sequence construction via concatenation of sequence blocks, with each rotated by a per-block phase offset, e.g., [R1-2605856]
B3717: Option 5: Sequence construction via concatenation of sequence blocks, without separate phase rotation per block, e.g., [R1-2605856]
B3718: Option 6: Enhancements to ZC sequence root selection, e.g., time-domain root selection for ZC-based sequence, e.g., [R1-2606029], configurability of  value by aNB , e.g.,  [R1-2605389], new ZC root determination equation for frequency domain ZC sequence mapping , e.g., [R1-2605838], [R1-2605570], [R1-2606508]
B3719: Option 7: ZCbased sequences as in NR, and expand the sequencegroup number (e.g., from 30 to 46, 58 or 70) and the basesequence number (e.g., from 2 to 3, 4, 5 or 6) to enlarge the SRS rootsequence pool, e.g., [R1-2605335]
B3720: Option 8: Polynomial phase sequence, e.g.,  , e.g., [R1-2605299]
B3721: Option 9: Phase optimized ZC sequence, i.e., based on Gerchberg-Saxton algorithm, e.g., [R1-2605962]
B3722: Option 10: Enhanced CGS with expanded phase-rotation alphabets (e.g., 64-PSK or higher) and/or with new lengths, e.g., [R1-2605469]
B3723: Option 11: ZC and CGS sequences in NR with bandwidth allocation≤272 RBs
B3724: Study how to support larger SRS bandwidth
B3726: Agreement
B3727: For SRS, at least cyclic-shift and/or comb-offset are supported.
B3729: Agreement
B3730: Study whether/how TD-OCC can be supported together with SRS repetition to enable multiplexing of SRS ports, and at least take the following aspects into consideration:
B3731: OCC lengths: e.g., TD-OCC2, TD-OCC4, other lengths
B3732: Whether it is applicable to intra- and inter-UE multiplexing of SRS ports
B3733: TD-OCC related interference randomization scheme
B3734: The non-orthogonality incurred by partial dropping
B3735: Channel variation in time domain
B3736: Agreement
B3737: For at least increasing SRS capacity/reducing overhead/improve coverage purposes sparse SRS patterns with comb values larger than 8, study at least comb 12 and 16 with consideration of at least the following aspects.
B3738: SRS sequences
B3739: MPR and PAPR/CM impact
B3740: Max number of cyclic shifts for the corresponding comb value
B3741: Minimum available bandwidth and its corresponding sequence
B3742: Channel estimation performance
B3743: Applicable RB sizes (e.g., RB size >= 8)
B3744: Any other performance impacts
B3745: FFS other comb values.
B3746: Note: RAN4 input on maximum power boosting is needed
B3748: Agreement
B3749: Support cross-slot SRS transmission at least for consecutive S+U slots
B3750: FFS: whether to support cross-slot SRS transmission spanning any two consecutive slots, e.g., U+U slots.
B3753: Agreement
B3754: Study at least the following aspects of precoded SRS
B3755: Intended usage (e.g., for UL CSI and/or DL CSI) 
B3756: Feasibility under specific UE architectures (e.g., xTyR configurations for DL CSI)
B3757: Gain compared to non-precoded SRS, accounting for practical impairments (e.g., PAPR/CM loss, realistic UE reciprocity, and UE coherency assumptions)
B3758: Methods for precoder derivation of precoded SRS:
B3759: Method-1: Determined by NW (e.g., derived from the measurements of SRS and indications from NW) 
B3760: Method-2: Determined (with/without reported) by UE via DL measurement 
B3761: FFS: UE UL/DL reciprocity calibration
B3764: Agreement
B3765: For SRS of 6GR, study the following frequency hopping aspects
B3766: A single-level frequency hopping
B3767: hopping over bandwidth with one or multiple frequency hops
B3768: Two-level frequency hopping
B3769: First level: hopping over BW with one or multiple frequency hops
B3770: Second level: hopping within each frequency hop of the first-level hopping 
B3771: For single or two levels, consider at least the following options
B3772: Nested tree-like frequency hopping pattern 
B3773: Sequential hopping pattern
B3774: Pattern following RB offset provided by NW 
B3775: With/without Intra-repetition hopping in the second level
B3776: Note: the case with larger DL BW than UL BW will be discussed separately 
B3778: Agreement
B3779: To support maximum SRS bandwidth beyond 272 PRBs in a carrier, consider the following options:
B3780: Option 1: Table based approach (similar to NR)
B3781: Option 1-1: 
B3782: Refer to the bandwidth entries for up to 272 PRBs in the table from NR
B3783: Introduce new SRS bandwidth entries from 272 to [544] PRBs
B3784: Option 1-2: 
B3785: Scale up the SRS bandwidth entries in the table from NR by integer factor, e.g. value of 2 or 4
B3786: Option 1-3: 
B3787: The overall SRS bandwidth is divided into more than one part, in which each part can reuse SRS bandwidth entries in the table from NR.
B3788: Option 2: Flexible SRS bandwidth determination and frequency hopping
B3789: Replace the existing table-based SRS bandwidth determination with parameter-based frequency hopping pattern determination provided by NW

B4189: For Option 2 of the physical channels/signals for the two stages in two-stage DCI study

B4542 R0: BS Rx configurations | Payload size and corresponding PUSCH RB allocation. | Waveform, Channel model and channel correlation assumptions | Configuration of DMRS port and DMRS sequence  | Distribution of SNR of different UEs | DMRS ports/scramble IDs among simultaneous UEs. e.g., UEs use different DMRS ports, UEs use same DMRS ports but different scramble IDs. | Evaluation results Note: company to report, for BLER results, whether blind detection on DMRS port and sequence is used or not.
B4542 R1:  |  |  |  |  |  | SNR loss @10% BLER for 2,3,4 UEs w.r.t single UE transmission | SNR loss @1% for 2,3,4 UEs w.r.t single UE transmission | Optional: BLER error floors for 2,3,4 UEs: yes or no, and if yes, starting SNR and level of BLER error floor. | Miss detection and false alarm rate of DMRS port or sequence.
B4545: Agreement
B4546: For the study of multi-bit SR for requesting UL resources in 6G:
B4547: Focus the study and evaluation on the following candidate reporting content:
B4548: Quantized buffer size.
B4549: Note: this candidate is for evaluation purpose only.
B4550: Study and determine whether/how the reported information in multi-bit SR can assist the BS to better schedule uplink resource for the UE
B4551: Study and determine for multi-bit SR:
B4552: Applicable and non-applicable scenarios for multi-bit SR, including cell load (low/high) and target packet size (small/large).
B4553: Whether and how to accommodate dynamic/flexible range of payload size of UL data.
B4554: Excess granted resource caused by buffer size quantization.
B4555: Study and evaluate the required/maximal bit size and study the quantization method. 
B4556: With the same aggregated UL resource overhead of multi-bit SR and 1-bit SR+ scheduled PUSCH for BSR, evaluate UL transmission scheduling latency performance of multi-SR vs the mechanism of 1-bit SR + BSR (the waiting time for next SR transmission occasion is included).
B4557: Evaluate the link coverage of multi-bit SR transmission in PUCCH with 1-bit SR coverage as baseline. 
B4558: Compare the multi-bit SR carrying quantized buffer size with the solution of using multiple 1-bit SR configurations to indicate the same target quantized buffer size, considering the resource overhead and UL transmission scheduling latency.
B4559: Other candidate reporting contents can also be evaluated
B4560: E.g., delay status report, LCH/LCG ID, predicted traffic timing information.
B4561: Agreement
B4562: For remote interference management for 6GR:
B4563: At least support wireless-based RIM framework specified in 5G/NR as starting point for 6GR.
B4564: Further study whether to support backhaul-based RIM framework.
B4565: Support the following two types of RIM-RS:
B4566: RIM-RS Type 1: transmitted by victim BS to indicate the existence of remote interference
B4567: RIM-RS Type 2: transmitted by aggressor BS to detect if remote interference still exists.
B4568: The RIM RS shall carry the following information:
B4569: BS group ID
B4570: ‘Enough/Not enough’ indication in RIM-RS Type 1
B4571: ‘Near/Far’ indication in RIM RS type 1.
B4572: FFS: the RIM RS can carry 6G specific information.
B4573: FFS: the RIM RS to carry duplex specific information, e.g., TDD frame structure, SBFD  subband/symbol configuration.
B4574: FFS the time-domain and frequency domain design of RIM RS, the sequence in RIM RS, and the configuration methods of RIM RS, time location of RIM RS.
B4575: FFS whether CSI-RS can be reused as RIM RS.

B4906: Whether/how to mitigate interference to the cell/carrier other than cell/carrier #2
B4907: Whether/how to handle the impact to UL WUS detection performance
B4908: Whether UE can determine path-loss estimation from cell/carrier #1 DL signals and power offset value provided by cell/carrier #1 when available/applicable.
B4909: Details on how to use path-loss estimation from cell/carrier #1 DL signals
B4910: UE can use wide/omni-directional beam or beam sweeping as UL WUS transmission beam. Further study
B4911: How to handle the impact of reduced cell coverage, when using wide/omni-directional beam.
B4912: How to determine narrow beam and whether/how to mitigate interference to the cell/carrier other than cell/carrier #2.
B4913: UE can use cell/carrier #1 SSB as timing reference for UL WUS transmission when cell/carrier #1 and cell/carrier #2 are synchronized when the timing can be maintained in UE, or when cell#1/carrier #1 provides underlay coverage for cell#2/carrier #2.
B4914: It is required to further study whether/how UE can use cell/carrier #1 SSB as timing reference for UL WUS transmission or not, when cell/carrier #1 and cell/carrier #2 are not synchronized, considering inter-cell distance, timing advance, etc.
B4915: This can cover the scenario where UE moves from cell/carrier #1 to cell/carrier #2 (where cell/carrier #1 may not provide underlay coverage for cell/carrier #2) and can maintain DL timing/frequency synchronization and UL WUS configuration for a certain period of time
B4916: It is required to further study the duration of the certain period of time with coordination with RAN4 as needed
B4917: Agreement

B4919: Time and frequency domain resource size and location of UL WUS

B4924: Cell access related information

B5105: R1-2606419Feature lead summary#2 on NTN specific requirements and designModerator (Qualcomm Incorporated)
B5107: Agreement:
B5108: The RAN1#125 agreement on LLS assumptions for SSB in 6GR NTN is corrected as follows:
B5109: The following parameters are used to evaluate SSB coverage for the 6GR NTN link budget template:
B5110: NOTE: Structure of NR signals / channels and other functionalities/features can be initially used for evaluation until other agenda items progress on the 6GR signals / channel structure/functionalities/features.
B5111 R0: Periodicity | To be reported
B5111 R1: Performance metric | 1% missed detection with  0. 1% false alarm rate for PSS/SSS. 1% BLER for PBCH.  Performance metric for different component physical channels / signals within the SSB (i.e., PSS, SSS, PBCH) can be separately reported Note: UE is not assumed to know the SS/PBCH block index
B5111 R2: SSB combining | Companies to report which component physical channels / signals within the SSB (i.e., PSS, SSS, PBCH) are combined, and how many SSBs are combined (if any). Single-shot SSB detection is also reported. 
B5111 R3: Other parameters | Reported by companies (including maximum CFO and timing drift rate (us/s) assumed, combining algorithm, etc). CFO and timing drift are mandatorily reported.

B5122: R1-2606420Feature lead summary#3 on NTN specific requirements and designModerator (Qualcomm Incorporated)
B5125: Agreement: 
B5126: For the 6GR NTN link budget template, for Ka/Ku band link level evaluations, the UE and SAN phase noise model in clause 6.1.11.2 of TR38.803 can be optionally used for BPSK/QPSK (and in that case is reported by companies) and is used for higher order modulation (if evaluated for the link budget template)
B5127: NOTE: This agreement only applies to the 6GR NTN link budget template.
B5128: Observation:
B5129: On the issue of HARQ stalling, the following observations are drawn:
B5130: On Solution 1 (HARQ feedback disabling) and Solution 3 (aNB reusing the same HARQ process before receiving HARQ-ACK feedback for the previous transmission):
B5131: RAN1 observes that solution 1 was specified in NR Rel-17, and it is clarified in TS 38.300 that “Disabling HARQ feedback allows scheduling a HARQ process before one HARQ RTT has elapsed since last scheduled”. This solution is feasible from RAN1 perspective
B5132: From RAN1 perspective, regarding Solution 3, it is feasible to allow usage of the same HARQ process before the HARQ-ACK feedback has been processed by the base station (subject to out-of-order rules at the UE). Current RAN2 specifications for NR, however, may prevent this behavior at least in some cases (e.g. when DRX is configured).
B5133: Under solution 3, having HARQ-ACK feedback for every downlink transmission incurs in signaling overhead and corresponding UE power consumption, which is considered by some companies to be unacceptable for some NTN scenarios. Solution 1 would alleviate this issue. 
B5134: Some companies indicate that the UE power consumption and overhead associated with HARQ-ACK transmission is limited, and that solution 1 removes the information on successful or unsuccessful reception of the TB and may result in recovery through RLC retransmissions which would increase signaling overhead, latency and corresponding UE power consumption. This assumes that, under solution 3, HARQ-ACK information is used for ARQ retransmissions without relying on RLC status report.
B5135: Some companies indicate that although Solution 3 does not remove the information on successful or unsuccessful reception of the TB, it will not be useful for triggering HARQ retransmissions, and therefore with solution 3 there is also a possibility of ending up in recovery through RLC retransmissions. This assumes that, under solution 3, HARQ-ACK information is not used for ARQ retransmissions without relying on RLC status report.
B5137: Under solution 3, although HARQ-ACK feedback is not used for HARQ retransmissions, some companies indicate that the HARQ-ACK information can be useful for e.g. link adaptation. Some other companies indicate that similar information can be conveyed in an alternative manner (e.g. statistical reporting, dynamic enabling/disabling of HARQ feedback under Solution 1, or disabling of HARQ feedback for some of the HARQ processes). 
B5138: Some companies indicate that the potential benefits of solution 1 in terms of signaling efficiency and power consumption are not directly related to HARQ stalling. Some companies indicate that solution 1 has been specified in NR to solve the problem of HARQ stalling.
B5139: NOTE: The fact that solution 3 and solution 1 are deemed to be “feasible” has no impact on which solution (if any) would be specified.

B5153: R1-2606422Feature lead summary#5 on NTN specific requirements and designModerator (Qualcomm Incorporated)
B5155: Working assumption: 
B5156: On the link budget template for 6GR NTN, the following values are used for Ka and Ku-band:
B5157: “(28a) Atmospheric loss (dB)”
B5158: For Ka-band GEO 20GHz (12.5 degree elevation): 1.2dB
B5159: For Ka band GEO 30GHz (12.5 degree elevation): 1.1dB
B5160: For Ku band GEO (12.5 degree elevation): 0.2dB
B5161: For Ka-band LEO (30 degree elevation): 0.5dB
B5162: For Ku-band LEO (30 degree elevation): 0.1dB
B5163: NOTE: These losses do not include rain loss. The values are obtained from ITU P.676 figures.
B5164: “(28b) Shadow fading margin (dB)” = 0
B5165: “(28c) Scintillation loss (dB)”
B5166: For GEO (12.5 degree elevation) Ka band, 1.1dB
B5167: For LEO (30 degree elevation) Ka band, 0.3dB
B5168: For GEO (12.5 degree elevation) Ku band, 0.3dB
B5169: For LEO (30 degree elevation) Ku band, 0.2dB
B5170: NOTE: This scintillation loss is due to tropospheric scintillation, as described in TR 38.811.
B5171: “(28d) Polarization loss (dB)” = 0dB 
B5172: This is due to matching polarizations between UE and SAN antenna
B5173: “(4b-2) / (11-b2) Scanning loss (dB)” is
B5174: Calculated based on elevation angle and antenna element pattern gain for both SAN and UE (if they use antenna arrays)
B5175: NOTE: RAN1 might need to revisit the agreed antenna element pattern gain.
B5176: “(20) Receiver implementation margin (dB)” = 1dB
B5177: “(27) Penetration margin” = 0dB
B5178: “(28e) Other losses” = 0dB
B5179: NOTE: The study of a particular scenario for the link budget template does not imply that, if RAN1 determines a coverage gap for that scenario, it will be studied or specified how to close it.

An antenna efficiency of 50% is assumed which results in an effective element max gain of 2dB. The resulting overall antenna gain (after applying the array factor) is  41.08 dB

B5208: Working assumptions:
B5209: On the link budget template for 6GR NTN, the following values are used for S-band:
B5210: “(28a) Atmospheric loss (dB)” = 0.1dB for LEO 30 degree elevation and 0.2dB for GEO 12.5 degree
B5211: “(28b) Shadow fading margin (dB)” = 3dB
B5212: Note: Additional losses due to e.g. light indoor/in-vehicle penetration loss may be considered under APC.
B5213: “(28c) Scintillation loss (dB)” = 2.2dB
B5214: This is for the evaluated elevation angles.
B5215: “(28d) Polarization loss (dB)” = 3dB
B5216: Assumes SAN antenna is circularly polarized and UE antenna is linearly polarized
B5217: “(4b-2) /  (11-b2) Scanning loss (dB)” is
B5218: Calculated based on elevation angle and antenna element pattern gain, e.g. ~4dB for 30 degree elevation angle in LEO 600, if the SAN antenna is modeled as an array.
B5219: NOTE: RAN1 might need to revisit the agreed antenna element pattern gain.
B5220: For UEs with omni antennas (11a)/(4c) “Boresight Antenna gain” is set to
B5221: -5.5dB as baseline. 
B5222: 0dB can be considered optionally.
B5223: “(20) Receiver implementation margin (dB)” = 1dB
B5224: “(27) Penetration margin” = 0dB
B5225: Note: Losses due to e.g. light indoor/in-vehicle penetration loss may be considered under APC.
B5226: “(28e) Other losses”: is set to:
B5227: For outdoor UEs, 0dB
B5228: For UEs in adverse propagation condition (APC): [a value between 6dB and 15dB]
B5229: This value will be decided in the next meeting based on input from companies (values and associated scenarios).
B5230: APC is only evaluated for downlink broadcast channel / signals.
B5231: NOTE: UEs in adverse propagation conditions is per RAN guidance and is documented in TR 38.914 as 0% of users in Case 1 and 4% of users in Case 2.
B5232: NOTE: The study of a particular scenario for the link budget template does not imply that, if RAN1 determines a coverage gap for that scenario, it will be studied or specified how to close it.

B5237: At least for LEO same orbit.
B5238: FFS other parameters (e.g. elevation angle)
